[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thanks. Um… honestly, what finally pushed me to make the appointment is that I keep dropping the ball on follow-through at work, and it’s getting really embarrassing.\n\nI’m an accountant, so after audits or after mentorship events, there are always these follow-up things — like sending the final email, adding a review note, completing a form, closing the loop on something that I genuinely did start. And I really do mean to finish them. I’ll have the tab open, or I’ll draft part of the message, or I’ll tell myself, “I’ll just clean this up and send it after this next thing,” and then somehow days go by and I find out it never actually got done.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care. That’s the part that’s been bothering me so much. I care a lot, and I’m usually trying really hard to stay on top of things. But then someone will ask, “Did that final review note get added?” or “Did you send that follow-up form?” and I get this horrible sinking feeling because I was sure I had handled it, or at least I meant to, and then I realize it’s just… unfinished. And it’s happened enough times now that I thought, okay, I probably need help figuring out why I keep doing this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while working on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. It’s honestly one of the main things that makes work feel harder than it probably should. Like if I’m reviewing an audit file, I can be genuinely trying to focus — I’ll have the workpaper open, I’ll be reading through the support, trying to check a number or make sure a note makes sense — and then a Slack ping comes in and it’s like my attention just snaps over there before I’ve even decided to look.\n\nOr someone talking outside my office, even if they’re not talking to me, can pull me completely out of what I was doing. And then I’m sitting there trying to remember, okay, where was I in this file, what was I verifying, what was the issue I was about to write down?\n\nSometimes it’s not even something external. I’ll suddenly think about marathon training — like whether I need to adjust a run or whether my shoes are getting worn out — and then I’m off thinking about that for a few minutes instead of the audit review. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to avoid the work. I’ll be in the middle of doing it, and my brain just… catches on something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Sitting still is actually kind of hard for me, especially in meetings. I’ll be trying to look like I’m paying attention and being normal, but under the table my foot is usually going constantly — like tapping or bouncing my leg — and I don’t always even realize I’m doing it until someone notices.\n\nI also click my pen a lot, or I’ll shift around in the chair, cross and uncross my legs, lean forward, lean back… it’s like I can’t just sit there quietly in one position. One of my colleagues has actually joked that she can hear my leg going under the table, which was funny but also a little embarrassing, because I thought I was being subtle. I’m not trying to be disruptive, it’s just sort of… my body does it when I’m expected to sit still for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with focus and restlessness, or did these issues start more recently?\n\nPatient: No, this definitely isn’t new. I mean, it feels like it’s become more costly now because I’m in a job where details and follow-through matter so much, but the pattern goes way back.\n\nEven in elementary school, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments or forgot to turn things in. I’d rush through work and miss small details, or need reminders to actually finish what I started. And I guess I talked out of turn too — that came up more than once.\n\nMy mom still jokes about it, actually. She’ll say I was the kind of kid who could ace the test, but the big project that was due would be sitting finished in my backpack because I forgot to hand it in. So… yes, the focus and restlessness pieces have been there for as long as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, and I’m not proud of it. It happens most at work, especially when I’m talking with junior auditors. They’ll be explaining an issue they found, or asking a question, and before they even finish laying it out, I can already see where they’re going — or I think I can — and the answer feels almost urgent in my head.\n\nSo I’ll jump in and say, “Oh, you need to tie that back to the support,” or “No, that variance is probably because of…” and then I realize they hadn’t finished. Sometimes they were actually about to add a detail that changes the whole thing, and I’ve already interrupted them. It’s like the thought comes up and my mouth starts before I’ve really made a decision to speak.\n\nI try to catch myself because I don’t want to come across as rude or dismissive. I really don’t. Especially with junior staff, I want them to feel comfortable asking questions and thinking through things out loud. But I can tell there are times where I cut them off because the idea is right there, and I feel this pressure to get it out before it disappears or before the conversation moves on. Then afterward I’m like, okay, that was unnecessary — I could have waited ten seconds.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like doing something sudden or spontaneous outside of speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, if you mean like big risky sudden things, I don’t know that I’d describe myself that way. I don’t have a clear example jumping to mind where I just suddenly did something dramatic without thinking.\n\nI can be spontaneous in smaller ways, I guess, but I’m not sure whether that’s what you’re asking about. Like, I’d have to think about it more, because the interrupting thing is obvious to me — other people react to it, and I notice it afterward. But with acting impulsively outside of talking, it’s harder for me to separate “normal spur-of-the-moment decision” from something that’s actually a problem.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure I’ve noticed that as a major issue, at least not in the same clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping your workspace or living space organized?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I wouldn’t say my space is always perfectly organized, but I also don’t know that I think of it as one of my main problems.\n\nAt work, I have piles sometimes, but in my head there’s a logic to them — like “this stack is things I still need to look at,” or “that folder is related to this client.” To someone else it might look messy, but I can usually explain why it’s there. And at home, things can accumulate on the counter or on a chair, but I don’t know… I kind of assume that happens to a lot of people.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a major issue, at least not compared with other things. If you mean something more specific by “organized,” I may need you to clarify.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to complete a task once it's been started, even if you're interested in it?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the main reasons I finally made this appointment, because it’s not just that I procrastinate starting things — I do start them. I’ll start the follow-up after an audit, or after a mentorship event, and I genuinely intend to finish it. I’ll have the email open, or the review note partly written, or the form sitting there, and in my mind it’s basically “in progress,” so it feels like it’s handled.\n\nBut then a few days later I’ll realize the final email never went out, or the review note was never completed, or the form is still sitting unfinished. And it’s frustrating because I’m not uninterested in the work. A lot of it matters to me, especially the follow-up with junior staff or making sure audit items are properly closed out. I care about doing it well. It’s more like I get it 70 or 80 percent of the way there, and then that last step somehow drops off.\n\nAnd then when I discover it, I feel embarrassed, because from the outside it probably looks careless. But at the time, I really did mean to finish it. I can remember starting it. I can remember thinking, “I’ll just clean this up and send it,” or “I need to finalize that note,” and then apparently I moved on before the task was actually complete. So yes, completing things once they’re started is definitely a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and completing tasks caused you any significant problems in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I wish I could say it’s mostly just annoying, but it has caused real problems.\n\nAt work it’s gotten to the point where it’s not just me feeling stressed internally. My mistakes and unfinished follow-up have actually affected things. There was a client review where something I hadn’t finished or followed through on properly became an issue, and that was really humiliating. And now, especially because I’m at the level where more is expected of me, I worry people are starting to question whether I can actually handle the promotion. Not necessarily because I don’t understand the work — I do — but because if I miss the follow-through or leave things half-closed, it makes me look unreliable.\n\nAnd at home, yes, it affects my husband. He’s had to take over bills and things I had agreed I would handle, because I’ll say, “Yes, I’ll do that,” and I mean it when I say it, but then it doesn’t get done reliably. So he ends up stepping in, not because he wants to control everything, but because he can’t really count on me to complete those things. That’s hard to admit, because I don’t want to be someone he has to manage. But yes, it’s definitely caused problems outside of work too.\n\nDoctor: Were you diagnosed with ADHD as a child?\n\nPatient: No, not that I know of. I don’t remember ever being evaluated for it or having anyone sit down with my parents and say there was a diagnosis like that.\n\nI mean, I was a kid a long time ago, and I’m sure there are things I don’t remember clearly, but as far as I know, no. It wasn’t something that was ever formally identified in childhood.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things on impulse, like suddenly quitting a task or activity?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if I’d describe it as suddenly quitting in a dramatic way, like storming off from something. It’s more that I sort of… leave a task before it’s actually finished, even though in my head I haven’t consciously decided to stop.\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to make this appointment. After audits or mentorship events, I’ll start the follow-up items with every intention of finishing them. I’ll open the email, start drafting the final message, begin the review note, or pull up the form that needs to be completed. And at that moment I’m genuinely thinking, “Yes, I’m doing this now, this is being handled.”\n\nBut then somehow I move on before the actual last step happens. Days later I’ll discover the final email never went out, or the review note is still half-finished, or the form was never actually submitted. So it’s not exactly that I consciously think, “I don’t want to do this anymore.” It’s more like I shift away from it too soon, almost automatically, and then I don’t realize until later that I never finished it.\n\nSo if that counts as quitting a task suddenly, then yes, that happens to me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do impulsive things physically, like suddenly getting up and leaving a room?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, although it’s usually not like I suddenly announce, “I’m leaving,” or make a big obvious exit. It’s more subtle than that.\n\nIf I’m in a long review or sitting through something where I’m expected to stay still for a while, I start to feel physically uncomfortable. It’s hard to explain — it’s like this pressure builds up inside me, almost like my body is telling me I need to move, even if there’s no real reason to. I’ll start shifting in my chair, thinking about how much longer I have to sit there, and then I’ll find some excuse to get up.\n\nLike I’ll say I need to go to the printer, or I’ll offer to grab something, or I’ll suddenly decide I need water, or I’ll check something across the room that probably could have waited. Sometimes I’m not even fully aware I’m doing it until I’m already standing. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude or escape the person I’m with — sitting still just starts to feel really uncomfortable, and moving gives me relief.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and completing tasks are more problematic for you than your restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think the focus and follow-through side is more problematic for me, honestly. The restlessness is uncomfortable and it can be annoying, but the thing that actually pushed me to make this appointment was realizing how often I’m starting work-related follow-up tasks and then somehow not actually completing them.\n\nAfter audits or mentorship events, I’ll begin the follow-up with every intention of finishing it. I’ll open the email, start the review note, pull up the form, whatever it is, and in my mind I’m handling it. But then days later I’ll discover the final email never got sent, or the review note is still sitting there unfinished, or the form was never submitted. And that’s the part that worries me, because it looks like I didn’t care or didn’t follow through, when really I thought I was taking care of it.\n\nSo yes, if I had to choose, the incomplete tasks and losing track of the final step are more disruptive for me than the physical restlessness.\n\nDoctor: Do you often engage in impulsive behaviors outside of work or speaking, such as sudden, unplanned purchases or reckless driving?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so — at least, not in a way that really stands out to me.\n\nWith purchases, I mean, I can buy something unplanned like anyone does, especially if I’m already online or in a store, but I wouldn’t say I’m making big sudden purchases that get me into trouble or anything like that. It’s not like I’m buying cars or expensive things on a whim. I might get some little thing and later think, “Did I really need that?” but it doesn’t feel like a major pattern.\n\nAnd reckless driving, no, I don’t really think of myself that way. I’m pretty cautious, honestly. I don’t have this sense that I’m taking big risks for the thrill of it.\n\nSo outside of work or conversation-type situations, I’m not sure I’d describe myself as especially impulsive. The issues I notice more are in the things I’ve already mentioned, where I move away from something or act before I’ve really thought it through in the moment.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

And at that moment I’m genuinely thinking, “Yes, I’m doing this now, this is being handled.”\n\nBut then somehow I move on before the actual last step happens. Days later I’ll discover the final email never went out, or the review note is still half-finished, or the form was never actually submitted. So it’s not exactly that I consciously think, “I don’t want to do this anymore.” It’s more like I shift away from it too soon, almost automatically, and then I don’t realize until later that I never finished it.\n\nSo if that counts as quitting a task suddenly, then yes, that happens to me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do impulsive things physically, like suddenly getting up and leaving a room?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, although it’s usually not like I suddenly announce, “I’m leaving,” or make a big obvious exit. It’s more subtle than that.\n\nIf I’m in a long review or sitting through something where I’m expected to stay still for a while, I start to feel physically uncomfortable. It’s hard to explain — it’s like this pressure builds up inside me, almost like my body is telling me I need to move, even if there’s no real reason to. I’ll start shifting in my chair, thinking about how much longer I have to sit there, and then I’ll find some excuse to get up.\n\nLike I’ll say I need to go to the printer, or I’ll offer to grab something, or I’ll suddenly decide I need water, or I’ll check something across the room that probably could have waited. Sometimes I’m not even fully aware I’m doing it until I’m already standing. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude or escape the person I’m with — sitting still just starts to feel really uncomfortable, and moving gives me relief.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and completing tasks are more problematic for you than your restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think the focus and follow-through side is more problematic for me, honestly. The restlessness is uncomfortable and it can be annoying, but the thing that actually pushed me to make this appointment was realizing how often I’m starting work-related follow-up tasks and then somehow not actually completing them.\n\nAfter audits or mentorship events, I’ll begin the follow-up with every intention of finishing it. I’ll open the email, start the review note, pull up the form, whatever it is, and in my mind I’m handling it. But then days later I’ll discover the final email never got sent, or the review note is still sitting there unfinished, or the form was never submitted. And that’s the part that worries me, because it looks like I didn’t care or didn’t follow through, when really I thought I was taking care of it.\n\nSo yes, if I had to choose, the incomplete tasks and losing track of the final step are more disruptive for me than the physical restlessness.\n\nDoctor: Do you often engage in impulsive behaviors outside of work or speaking, such as sudden, unplanned purchases or reckless driving?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so — at least, not in a way that really stands out to me.\n\nWith purchases, I mean, I can buy something unplanned like anyone does, especially if I’m already online or in a store, but I wouldn’t say I’m making big sudden purchases that get me into trouble or anything like that. It’s not like I’m buying cars or expensive things on a whim. I might get some little thing and later think, “Did I really need that?” but it doesn’t feel like a major pattern.\n\nAnd reckless driving, no, I don’t really think of myself that way. I’m pretty cautious, honestly. I don’t have this sense that I’m taking big risks for the thrill of it.\n\nSo outside of work or conversation-type situations, I’m not sure I’d describe myself as especially impulsive. The issues I notice more are in the things I’ve already mentioned, where I move away from something or act before I’ve really thought it through in the moment.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… I think it is, unfortunately. I don’t really like saying that, because for a long time I sort of told myself it was just stress or being busy, or that everyone drops things sometimes. But it’s gotten to the point where it’s not just a private annoyance anymore.

At work, the unfinished follow-up and little mistakes have started to have real consequences. There was a client review that was affected because I didn’t complete something the way I thought I had, or I left a final step hanging, and that was really upsetting. I keep worrying that people are looking at me differently now, like they’re wondering whether I can actually handle the promotion or the level of responsibility I’m supposed to be taking on. And I hate that, because I do care, and I’m not careless in the sense of not trying. But from the outside, I know it can look like I’m unreliable.

And at home it’s affecting things too. My husband has had to take over bills and commitments that I agreed to handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. I’ll say, “Yes, I’ll take care of that,” and I mean it when I say it, but then it doesn’t always get done, or it gets done late, or I need reminders. That creates tension, because I think he feels like he has to manage me, and I feel embarrassed and defensive because I don’t want to be that person.

So yes, I would say it’s significant. It’s affecting how people rely on me, both at work and at home.

    "role": "system",

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thanks. Um… honestly, what finally pushed me to make the appointment is that I keep dropping the ball on follow-through at work, and it’s getting really embarrassing.\n\nI’m an accountant, so after audits or after mentorship events, there are always these follow-up things — like sending the final email, adding a review note, completing a form, closing the loop on something that I genuinely did start. And I really do mean to finish them. I’ll have the tab open, or I’ll draft part of the message, or I’ll tell myself, “I’ll just clean this up and send it after this next thing,” and then somehow days go by and I find out it never actually got done.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care. That’s the part that’s been bothering me so much. I care a lot, and I’m usually trying really hard to stay on top of things. But then someone will ask, “Did that final review note get added?” or “Did you send that follow-up form?” and I get this horrible sinking feeling because I was sure I had handled it, or at least I meant to, and then I realize it’s just… unfinished. And it’s happened enough times now that I thought, okay, I probably need help figuring out why I keep doing this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while working on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. It’s honestly one of the main things that makes work feel harder than it probably should. Like if I’m reviewing an audit file, I can be genuinely trying to focus — I’ll have the workpaper open, I’ll be reading through the support, trying to check a number or make sure a note makes sense — and then a Slack ping comes in and it’s like my attention just snaps over there before I’ve even decided to look.\n\nOr someone talking outside my office, even if they’re not talking to me, can pull me completely out of what I was doing. And then I’m sitting there trying to remember, okay, where was I in this file, what was I verifying, what was the issue I was about to write down?\n\nSometimes it’s not even something external. I’ll suddenly think about marathon training — like whether I need to adjust a run or whether my shoes are getting worn out — and then I’m off thinking about that for a few minutes instead of the audit review. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to avoid the work. I’ll be in the middle of doing it, and my brain just… catches on something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Sitting still is actually kind of hard for me, especially in meetings. I’ll be trying to look like I’m paying attention and being normal, but under the table my foot is usually going constantly — like tapping or bouncing my leg — and I don’t always even realize I’m doing it until someone notices.\n\nI also click my pen a lot, or I’ll shift around in the chair, cross and uncross my legs, lean forward, lean back… it’s like I can’t just sit there quietly in one position. One of my colleagues has actually joked that she can hear my leg going under the table, which was funny but also a little embarrassing, because I thought I was being subtle. I’m not trying to be disruptive, it’s just sort of… my body does it when I’m expected to sit still for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with focus and restlessness, or did these issues start more recently?\n\nPatient: No, this definitely isn’t new. I mean, it feels like it’s become more costly now because I’m in a job where details and follow-through matter so much, but the pattern goes way back.\n\nEven in elementary school, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments or forgot to turn things in. I’d rush through work and miss small details, or need reminders to actually finish what I started. And I guess I talked out of turn too — that came up more than once.\n\nMy mom still jokes about it, actually. She’ll say I was the kind of kid who could ace the test, but the big project that was due would be sitting finished in my backpack because I forgot to hand it in. So… yes, the focus and restlessness pieces have been there for as long as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, and I’m not proud of it. It happens most at work, especially when I’m talking with junior auditors. They’ll be explaining an issue they found, or asking a question, and before they even finish laying it out, I can already see where they’re going — or I think I can — and the answer feels almost urgent in my head.\n\nSo I’ll jump in and say, “Oh, you need to tie that back to the support,” or “No, that variance is probably because of…” and then I realize they hadn’t finished. Sometimes they were actually about to add a detail that changes the whole thing, and I’ve already interrupted them. It’s like the thought comes up and my mouth starts before I’ve really made a decision to speak.\n\nI try to catch myself because I don’t want to come across as rude or dismissive. I really don’t. Especially with junior staff, I want them to feel comfortable asking questions and thinking through things out loud. But I can tell there are times where I cut them off because the idea is right there, and I feel this pressure to get it out before it disappears or before the conversation moves on. Then afterward I’m like, okay, that was unnecessary — I could have waited ten seconds.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like doing something sudden or spontaneous outside of speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, if you mean like big risky sudden things, I don’t know that I’d describe myself that way. I don’t have a clear example jumping to mind where I just suddenly did something dramatic without thinking.\n\nI can be spontaneous in smaller ways, I guess, but I’m not sure whether that’s what you’re asking about. Like, I’d have to think about it more, because the interrupting thing is obvious to me — other people react to it, and I notice it afterward. But with acting impulsively outside of talking, it’s harder for me to separate “normal spur-of-the-moment decision” from something that’s actually a problem.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure I’ve noticed that as a major issue, at least not in the same clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping your workspace or living space organized?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I wouldn’t say my space is always perfectly organized, but I also don’t know that I think of it as one of my main problems.\n\nAt work, I have piles sometimes, but in my head there’s a logic to them — like “this stack is things I still need to look at,” or “that folder is related to this client.” To someone else it might look messy, but I can usually explain why it’s there. And at home, things can accumulate on the counter or on a chair, but I don’t know… I kind of assume that happens to a lot of people.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a major issue, at least not compared with other things. If you mean something more specific by “organized,” I may need you to clarify.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to complete a task once it's been started, even if you're interested in it?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the main reasons I finally made this appointment, because it’s not just that I procrastinate starting things — I do start them. I’ll start the follow-up after an audit, or after a mentorship event, and I genuinely intend to finish it. I’ll have the email open, or the review note partly written, or the form sitting there, and in my mind it’s basically “in progress,” so it feels like it’s handled.\n\nBut then a few days later I’ll realize the final email never went out, or the review note was never completed, or the form is still sitting unfinished. And it’s frustrating because I’m not uninterested in the work. A lot of it matters to me, especially the follow-up with junior staff or making sure audit items are properly closed out. I care about doing it well. It’s more like I get it 70 or 80 percent of the way there, and then that last step somehow drops off.\n\nAnd then when I discover it, I feel embarrassed, because from the outside it probably looks careless. But at the time, I really did mean to finish it. I can remember starting it. I can remember thinking, “I’ll just clean this up and send it,” or “I need to finalize that note,” and then apparently I moved on before the task was actually complete. So yes, completing things once they’re started is definitely a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and completing tasks caused you any significant problems in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I wish I could say it’s mostly just annoying, but it has caused real problems.\n\nAt work it’s gotten to the point where it’s not just me feeling stressed internally. My mistakes and unfinished follow-up have actually affected things. There was a client review where something I hadn’t finished or followed through on properly became an issue, and that was really humiliating. And now, especially because I’m at the level where more is expected of me, I worry people are starting to question whether I can actually handle the promotion. Not necessarily because I don’t understand the work — I do — but because if I miss the follow-through or leave things half-closed, it makes me look unreliable.\n\nAnd at home, yes, it affects my husband. He’s had to take over bills and things I had agreed I would handle, because I’ll say, “Yes, I’ll do that,” and I mean it when I say it, but then it doesn’t get done reliably. So he ends up stepping in, not because he wants to control everything, but because he can’t really count on me to complete those things. That’s hard to admit, because I don’t want to be someone he has to manage. But yes, it’s definitely caused problems outside of work too.\n\nDoctor: Were you diagnosed with ADHD as a child?\n\nPatient: No, not that I know of. I don’t remember ever being evaluated for it or having anyone sit down with my parents and say there was a diagnosis like that.\n\nI mean, I was a kid a long time ago, and I’m sure there are things I don’t remember clearly, but as far as I know, no. It wasn’t something that was ever formally identified in childhood.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things on impulse, like suddenly quitting a task or activity?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if I’d describe it as suddenly quitting in a dramatic way, like storming off from something. It’s more that I sort of… leave a task before it’s actually finished, even though in my head I haven’t consciously decided to stop.\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to make this appointment. After audits or mentorship events, I’ll start the follow-up items with every intention of finishing them. I’ll open the email, start drafting the final message, begin the review note, or pull up the form that needs to be completed. And at that moment I’m genuinely thinking, “Yes, I’m doing this now, this is being handled.”\n\nBut then somehow I move on before the actual last step happens. Days later I’ll discover the final email never went out, or the review note is still half-finished, or the form was never actually submitted. So it’s not exactly that I consciously think, “I don’t want to do this anymore.” It’s more like I shift away from it too soon, almost automatically, and then I don’t realize until later that I never finished it.\n\nSo if that counts as quitting a task suddenly, then yes, that happens to me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do impulsive things physically, like suddenly getting up and leaving a room?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, although it’s usually not like I suddenly announce, “I’m leaving,” or make a big obvious exit. It’s more subtle than that.\n\nIf I’m in a long review or sitting through something where I’m expected to stay still for a while, I start to feel physically uncomfortable. It’s hard to explain — it’s like this pressure builds up inside me, almost like my body is telling me I need to move, even if there’s no real reason to. I’ll start shifting in my chair, thinking about how much longer I have to sit there, and then I’ll find some excuse to get up.\n\nLike I’ll say I need to go to the printer, or I’ll offer to grab something, or I’ll suddenly decide I need water, or I’ll check something across the room that probably could have waited. Sometimes I’m not even fully aware I’m doing it until I’m already standing. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude or escape the person I’m with — sitting still just starts to feel really uncomfortable, and moving gives me relief.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and completing tasks are more problematic for you than your restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think the focus and follow-through side is more problematic for me, honestly. The restlessness is uncomfortable and it can be annoying, but the thing that actually pushed me to make this appointment was realizing how often I’m starting work-related follow-up tasks and then somehow not actually completing them.\n\nAfter audits or mentorship events, I’ll begin the follow-up with every intention of finishing it. I’ll open the email, start the review note, pull up the form, whatever it is, and in my mind I’m handling it. But then days later I’ll discover the final email never got sent, or the review note is still sitting there unfinished, or the form was never submitted. And that’s the part that worries me, because it looks like I didn’t care or didn’t follow through, when really I thought I was taking care of it.\n\nSo yes, if I had to choose, the incomplete tasks and losing track of the final step are more disruptive for me than the physical restlessness.\n\nDoctor: Do you often engage in impulsive behaviors outside of work or speaking, such as sudden, unplanned purchases or reckless driving?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so — at least, not in a way that really stands out to me.\n\nWith purchases, I mean, I can buy something unplanned like anyone does, especially if I’m already online or in a store, but I wouldn’t say I’m making big sudden purchases that get me into trouble or anything like that. It’s not like I’m buying cars or expensive things on a whim. I might get some little thing and later think, “Did I really need that?” but it doesn’t feel like a major pattern.\n\nAnd reckless driving, no, I don’t really think of myself that way. I’m pretty cautious, honestly. I don’t have this sense that I’m taking big risks for the thrill of it.\n\nSo outside of work or conversation-type situations, I’m not sure I’d describe myself as especially impulsive. The issues I notice more are in the things I’ve already mentioned, where I move away from something or act before I’ve really thought it through in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity are interfering with your daily life and relationships to a significant degree?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, unfortunately. I don’t really like saying that, because for a long time I sort of told myself it was just stress or being busy, or that everyone drops things sometimes. But it’s gotten to the point where it’s not just a private annoyance anymore.\n\nAt work, the unfinished follow-up and little mistakes have started to have real consequences. There was a client review that was affected because I didn’t complete something the way I thought I had, or I left a final step hanging, and that was really upsetting. I keep worrying that people are looking at me differently now, like they’re wondering whether I can actually handle the promotion or the level of responsibility I’m supposed to be taking on. And I hate that, because I do care, and I’m not careless in the sense of not trying. But from the outside, I know it can look like I’m unreliable.\n\nAnd at home it’s affecting things too. My husband has had to take over bills and commitments that I agreed to handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. I’ll say, “Yes, I’ll take care of that,” and I mean it when I say it, but then it doesn’t always get done, or it gets done late, or I need reminders. That creates tension, because I think he feels like he has to manage me, and I feel embarrassed and defensive because I don’t want to be that person.\n\nSo yes, I would say it’s significant. It’s affecting how people rely on me, both at work and at home.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

No, I don’t think so. I mean… not the end of a significant relationship, no.

There have definitely been strains, especially with my husband, where he gets frustrated or feels like he can’t rely on me for certain things, and I can see how that wears on him. But it hasn’t led to a breakup or divorce or anything like that. And looking back on past relationships, I can’t point to one and say, “That ended because of these issues.” I’m sure some of my habits probably annoyed people or caused friction, but I wouldn’t say they were the main reason a significant relationship ended.

So, no — relationship tension, yes, but not an actual relationship ending because of it, at least not that I’m aware of.

That’s the part that’s been bothering me so much. I care a lot, and I’m usually trying really hard to stay on top of things. Sitting still is actually kind of hard for me, especially in meetings. I’ll be trying to look like I’m paying attention and being normal, but under the table my foot is usually going constantly — like tapping or bouncing my leg — and I don’t always even realize I’m doing it until someone notices.\n\nI also click my pen a lot, or I’ll shift around in the chair, cross and uncross my legs, lean forward, lean back… it’s like I can’t just sit there quietly in one position. One of my colleagues has actually joked that she can hear my leg going under the table, which was funny but also a little embarrassing, because I thought I was being subtle. I’m not trying to be disruptive, it’s just sort of… my body does it when I’m expected to sit still for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with focus and restlessness, or did these issues start more recently?\n\nPatient: No, this definitely isn’t new. I mean, it feels like it’s become more costly now because I’m in a job where details and follow-through matter so much, but the pattern goes way back.\n\nEven in elementary school, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments or forgot to turn things in. I’d rush through work and miss small details, or need reminders to actually finish what I started. And I guess I talked out of turn too — that came up more than once.\n\nMy mom still jokes about it, actually. She’ll say I was the kind of kid who could ace the test, but the big project that was due would be sitting finished in my backpack because I forgot to hand it in. So… yes, the focus and restlessness pieces have been there for as long as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, and I’m not proud of it. It happens most at work, especially when I’m talking with junior auditors. They’ll be explaining an issue they found, or asking a question, and before they even finish laying it out, I can already see where they’re going — or I think I can — and the answer feels almost urgent in my head.\n\nSo I’ll jump in and say, “Oh, you need to tie that back to the support,” or “No, that variance is probably because of…” and then I realize they hadn’t finished. Sometimes they were actually about to add a detail that changes the whole thing, and I’ve already interrupted them. It’s like the thought comes up and my mouth starts before I’ve really made a decision to speak.\n\nI try to catch myself because I don’t want to come across as rude or dismissive. I really don’t. Especially with junior staff, I want them to feel comfortable asking questions and thinking through things out loud. But I can tell there are times where I cut them off because the idea is right there, and I feel this pressure to get it out before it disappears or before the conversation moves on. Then afterward I’m like, okay, that was unnecessary — I could have waited ten seconds.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like doing something sudden or spontaneous outside of speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. And at home, things can accumulate on the counter or on a chair, but I don’t know… I kind of assume that happens to a lot of people.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a major issue, at least not compared with other things. If you mean something more specific by “organized,” I may need you to clarify.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to complete a task once it's been started, even if you're interested in it?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the main reasons I finally made this appointment, because it’s not just that I procrastinate starting things — I do start them. I’ll start the follow-up after an audit, or after a mentorship event, and I genuinely intend to finish it. I’ll have the email open, or the review note partly written, or the form sitting there, and in my mind it’s basically “in progress,” so it feels like it’s handled.\n\nBut then a few days later I’ll realize the final email never went out, or the review note was never completed, or the form is still sitting unfinished. My mistakes and unfinished follow-up have actually affected things. There was a client review where something I hadn’t finished or followed through on properly became an issue, and that was really humiliating. And now, especially because I’m at the level where more is expected of me, I worry people are starting to question whether I can actually handle the promotion. Not necessarily because I don’t understand the work — I do — but because if I miss the follow-through or leave things half-closed, it makes me look unreliable.\n\nAnd at home, yes, it affects my husband. That’s hard to admit, because I don’t want to be someone he has to manage. But yes, it’s definitely caused problems outside of work too.\n\nDoctor: Were you diagnosed with ADHD as a child?\n\nPatient: No, not that I know of. I don’t remember ever being evaluated for it or having anyone sit down with my parents and say there was a diagnosis like that.\n\nI mean, I was a kid a long time ago, and I’m sure there are things I don’t remember clearly, but as far as I know, no. It’s more subtle than that.\n\nIf I’m in a long review or sitting through something where I’m expected to stay still for a while, I start to feel physically uncomfortable. It’s hard to explain — it’s like this pressure builds up inside me, almost like my body is telling me I need to move, even if there’s no real reason to. I’ll start shifting in my chair, thinking about how much longer I have to sit there, and then I’ll find some excuse to get up.\n\nLike I’ll say I need to go to the printer, or I’ll offer to grab something, or I’ll suddenly decide I need water, or I’ll check something across the room that probably could have waited. Sometimes I’m not even fully aware I’m doing it until I’m already standing. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude or escape the person I’m with — sitting still just starts to feel really uncomfortable, and moving gives me relief.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and completing tasks are more problematic for you than your restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think the focus and follow-through side is more problematic for me, honestly. I’ll open the email, start the review note, pull up the form, whatever it is, and in my mind I’m handling it. But then days later I’ll discover the final email never got sent, or the review note is still sitting there unfinished, or the form was never submitted. And that’s the part that worries me, because it looks like I didn’t care or didn’t follow through, when really I thought I was taking care of it.\n\nSo yes, if I had to choose, the incomplete tasks and losing track of the final step are more disruptive for me than the physical restlessness.\n\nDoctor: Do you often engage in impulsive behaviors outside of work or speaking, such as sudden, unplanned purchases or reckless driving?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so — at least, not in a way that really stands out to me.\n\nWith purchases, I mean, I can buy something unplanned like anyone does, especially if I’m already online or in a store, but I wouldn’t say I’m making big sudden purchases that get me into trouble or anything like that. It’s not like I’m buying cars or expensive things on a whim. I might get some little thing and later think, “Did I really need that?” but it doesn’t feel like a major pattern.\n\nAnd reckless driving, no, I don’t really think of myself that way. I’m pretty cautious, honestly. I don’t have this sense that I’m taking big risks for the thrill of it.\n\nSo outside of work or conversation-type situations, I’m not sure I’d describe myself as especially impulsive. The issues I notice more are in the things I’ve already mentioned, where I move away from something or act before I’ve really thought it through in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity are interfering with your daily life and relationships to a significant degree?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, unfortunately. I don’t really like saying that, because for a long time I sort of told myself it was just stress or being busy, or that everyone drops things sometimes. But it’s gotten to the point where it’s not just a private annoyance anymore.\n\nAt work, the unfinished follow-up and little mistakes have started to have real consequences. There was a client review that was affected because I didn’t complete something the way I thought I had, or I left a final step hanging, and that was really upsetting. I keep worrying that people are looking at me differently now, like they’re wondering whether I can actually handle the promotion or the level of responsibility I’m supposed to be taking on. And I hate that, because I do care, and I’m not careless in the sense of not trying. But from the outside, I know it can look like I’m unreliable.\n\nAnd at home it’s affecting things too. My husband has had to take over bills and commitments that I agreed to handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. I’ll say, “Yes, I’ll take care of that,” and I mean it when I say it, but then it doesn’t always get done, or it gets done late, or I need reminders. That creates tension, because I think he feels like he has to manage me, and I feel embarrassed and defensive because I don’t want to be that person.\n\nSo yes, I would say it’s significant. It’s affecting how people rely on me, both at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever led to the end of a significant relationship?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean… not the end of a significant relationship, no.\n\nThere have definitely been strains, especially with my husband, where he gets frustrated or feels like he can’t rely on me for certain things, and I can see how that wears on him. But it hasn’t led to a breakup or divorce or anything like that. And looking back on past relationships, I can’t point to one and say, “That ended because of these issues.” I’m sure some of my habits probably annoyed people or caused friction, but I wouldn’t say they were the main reason a significant relationship ended.\n\nSo, no — relationship tension, yes, but not an actual relationship ending because of it, at least not that I’m aware of.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think so. I mean, when I look back, it was there pretty early, even in elementary school.

My report cards would say things like I was bright, or that I understood the material, but then there would always be these comments about needing reminders, or not finishing work, or losing assignments. It wasn’t that I couldn’t do the work — that’s what was confusing, I think, for my teachers and my parents. I could do well on a test if I was sitting there in front of it, but then I’d forget to turn in a project that was literally in my backpack, or I’d rush through something and miss small directions.

My mother still jokes about that, actually — that I could ace a test but forget the assignment sitting right there with me. At the time it probably sounded funny, but it was also frustrating because I’d get in trouble for things that seemed careless, and I didn’t really know how to explain that I hadn’t meant to ignore the instructions or lose the paper.

And I also remember being told I talked out of turn, or needed to be reminded to finish what I was working on. So yes, paying attention to the whole process — not just understanding the lesson, but following all the steps and details — was definitely an issue when I was a child.

I don’t have a clear example jumping to mind where I just suddenly did something dramatic without thinking.\n\nI can be spontaneous in smaller ways, I guess, but I’m not sure whether that’s what you’re asking about. And at home, things can accumulate on the counter or on a chair, but I don’t know… I kind of assume that happens to a lot of people.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a major issue, at least not compared with other things. I’ll have the email open, or the review note partly written, or the form sitting there, and in my mind it’s basically “in progress,” so it feels like it’s handled.\n\nBut then a few days later I’ll realize the final email never went out, or the review note was never completed, or the form is still sitting unfinished. Um… honestly, what finally pushed me to make the appointment is that I keep dropping the ball on follow-through at work, and it’s getting really embarrassing.\n\nI’m an accountant, so after audits or after mentorship events, there are always these follow-up things — like sending the final email, adding a review note, completing a form, closing the loop on something that I genuinely did start. And I really do mean to finish them. I’ll have the tab open, or I’ll draft part of the message, or I’ll tell myself, “I’ll just clean this up and send it after this next thing,” and then somehow days go by and I find out it never actually got done.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care. That’s the part that’s been bothering me so much. I care a lot, and I’m usually trying really hard to stay on top of things. But then someone will ask, “Did that final review note get added?” or “Did you send that follow-up form?” and I get this horrible sinking feeling because I was sure I had handled it, or at least I meant to, and then I realize it’s just… unfinished. And it’s happened enough times now that I thought, okay, I probably need help figuring out why I keep doing this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while working on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. It’s honestly one of the main things that makes work feel harder than it probably should. Like if I’m reviewing an audit file, I can be genuinely trying to focus — I’ll have the workpaper open, I’ll be reading through the support, trying to check a number or make sure a note makes sense — and then a Slack ping comes in and it’s like my attention just snaps over there before I’ve even decided to look.\n\nOr someone talking outside my office, even if they’re not talking to me, can pull me completely out of what I was doing. And then I’m sitting there trying to remember, okay, where was I in this file, what was I verifying, what was the issue I was about to write down?\n\nSometimes it’s not even something external. I’ll suddenly think about marathon training — like whether I need to adjust a run or whether my shoes are getting worn out — and then I’m off thinking about that for a few minutes instead of the audit review. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to avoid the work. I’ll be in the middle of doing it, and my brain just… catches on something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Sitting still is actually kind of hard for me, especially in meetings. I’ll be trying to look like I’m paying attention and being normal, but under the table my foot is usually going constantly — like tapping or bouncing my leg — and I don’t always even realize I’m doing it until someone notices.\n\nI also click my pen a lot, or I’ll shift around in the chair, cross and uncross my legs, lean forward, lean back… it’s like I can’t just sit there quietly in one position. One of my colleagues has actually joked that she can hear my leg going under the table, which was funny but also a little embarrassing, because I thought I was being subtle. I’m not trying to be disruptive, it’s just sort of… my body does it when I’m expected to sit still for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with focus and restlessness, or did these issues start more recently?\n\nPatient: No, this definitely isn’t new. I mean, it feels like it’s become more costly now because I’m in a job where details and follow-through matter so much, but the pattern goes way back.\n\nEven in elementary school, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments or forgot to turn things in. I’d rush through work and miss small details, or need reminders to actually finish what I started. And I guess I talked out of turn too — that came up more than once.\n\nMy mom still jokes about it, actually. She’ll say I was the kind of kid who could ace the test, but the big project that was due would be sitting finished in my backpack because I forgot to hand it in. So… yes, the focus and restlessness pieces have been there for as long as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, and I’m not proud of it. It happens most at work, especially when I’m talking with junior auditors. They’ll be explaining an issue they found, or asking a question, and before they even finish laying it out, I can already see where they’re going — or I think I can — and the answer feels almost urgent in my head.\n\nSo I’ll jump in and say, “Oh, you need to tie that back to the support,” or “No, that variance is probably because of…” and then I realize they hadn’t finished. Sometimes they were actually about to add a detail that changes the whole thing, and I’ve already interrupted them. It’s like the thought comes up and my mouth starts before I’ve really made a decision to speak.\n\nI try to catch myself because I don’t want to come across as rude or dismissive. I really don’t. Especially with junior staff, I want them to feel comfortable asking questions and thinking through things out loud. But I can tell there are times where I cut them off because the idea is right there, and I feel this pressure to get it out before it disappears or before the conversation moves on. Then afterward I’m like, okay, that was unnecessary — I could have waited ten seconds.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like doing something sudden or spontaneous outside of speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, if you mean like big risky sudden things, I don’t know that I’d describe myself that way. I don’t have a clear example jumping to mind where I just suddenly did something dramatic without thinking.\n\nI can be spontaneous in smaller ways, I guess, but I’m not sure whether that’s what you’re asking about. Like, I’d have to think about it more, because the interrupting thing is obvious to me — other people react to it, and I notice it afterward. But with acting impulsively outside of talking, it’s harder for me to separate “normal spur-of-the-moment decision” from something that’s actually a problem.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure I’ve noticed that as a major issue, at least not in the same clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping your workspace or living space organized?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I wouldn’t say my space is always perfectly organized, but I also don’t know that I think of it as one of my main problems.\n\nAt work, I have piles sometimes, but in my head there’s a logic to them — like “this stack is things I still need to look at,” or “that folder is related to this client.” To someone else it might look messy, but I can usually explain why it’s there. And at home, things can accumulate on the counter or on a chair, but I don’t know… I kind of assume that happens to a lot of people.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a major issue, at least not compared with other things. If you mean something more specific by “organized,” I may need you to clarify.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to complete a task once it's been started, even if you're interested in it?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the main reasons I finally made this appointment, because it’s not just that I procrastinate starting things — I do start them. I’ll start the follow-up after an audit, or after a mentorship event, and I genuinely intend to finish it. I’ll have the email open, or the review note partly written, or the form sitting there, and in my mind it’s basically “in progress,” so it feels like it’s handled.\n\nBut then a few days later I’ll realize the final email never went out, or the review note was never completed, or the form is still sitting unfinished. And it’s frustrating because I’m not uninterested in the work. A lot of it matters to me, especially the follow-up with junior staff or making sure audit items are properly closed out. I care about doing it well. It’s more like I get it 70 or 80 percent of the way there, and then that last step somehow drops off.\n\nAnd then when I discover it, I feel embarrassed, because from the outside it probably looks careless. But at the time, I really did mean to finish it. I can remember starting it. I can remember thinking, “I’ll just clean this up and send it,” or “I need to finalize that note,” and then apparently I moved on before the task was actually complete. So yes, completing things once they’re started is definitely a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and completing tasks caused you any significant problems in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I wish I could say it’s mostly just annoying, but it has caused real problems.\n\nAt work it’s gotten to the point where it’s not just me feeling stressed internally. My mistakes and unfinished follow-up have actually affected things. There was a client review where something I hadn’t finished or followed through on properly became an issue, and that was really humiliating. And now, especially because I’m at the level where more is expected of me, I worry people are starting to question whether I can actually handle the promotion. Not necessarily because I don’t understand the work — I do — but because if I miss the follow-through or leave things half-closed, it makes me look unreliable.\n\nAnd at home, yes, it affects my husband. He’s had to take over bills and things I had agreed I would handle, because I’ll say, “Yes, I’ll do that,” and I mean it when I say it, but then it doesn’t get done reliably. So he ends up stepping in, not because he wants to control everything, but because he can’t really count on me to complete those things. That’s hard to admit, because I don’t want to be someone he has to manage. But yes, it’s definitely caused problems outside of work too.\n\nDoctor: Were you diagnosed with ADHD as a child?\n\nPatient: No, not that I know of. I don’t remember ever being evaluated for it or having anyone sit down with my parents and say there was a diagnosis like that.\n\nI mean, I was a kid a long time ago, and I’m sure there are things I don’t remember clearly, but as far as I know, no. It wasn’t something that was ever formally identified in childhood.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things on impulse, like suddenly quitting a task or activity?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if I’d describe it as suddenly quitting in a dramatic way, like storming off from something. It’s more that I sort of… leave a task before it’s actually finished, even though in my head I haven’t consciously decided to stop.\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to make this appointment. After audits or mentorship events, I’ll start the follow-up items with every intention of finishing them. I’ll open the email, start drafting the final message, begin the review note, or pull up the form that needs to be completed. And at that moment I’m genuinely thinking, “Yes, I’m doing this now, this is being handled.”\n\nBut then somehow I move on before the actual last step happens. Days later I’ll discover the final email never went out, or the review note is still half-finished, or the form was never actually submitted. So it’s not exactly that I consciously think, “I don’t want to do this anymore.” It’s more like I shift away from it too soon, almost automatically, and then I don’t realize until later that I never finished it.\n\nSo if that counts as quitting a task suddenly, then yes, that happens to me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do impulsive things physically, like suddenly getting up and leaving a room?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, although it’s usually not like I suddenly announce, “I’m leaving,” or make a big obvious exit. It’s more subtle than that.\n\nIf I’m in a long review or sitting through something where I’m expected to stay still for a while, I start to feel physically uncomfortable. It’s hard to explain — it’s like this pressure builds up inside me, almost like my body is telling me I need to move, even if there’s no real reason to. I’ll start shifting in my chair, thinking about how much longer I have to sit there, and then I’ll find some excuse to get up.\n\nLike I’ll say I need to go to the printer, or I’ll offer to grab something, or I’ll suddenly decide I need water, or I’ll check something across the room that probably could have waited. Sometimes I’m not even fully aware I’m doing it until I’m already standing. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude or escape the person I’m with — sitting still just starts to feel really uncomfortable, and moving gives me relief.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and completing tasks are more problematic for you than your restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think the focus and follow-through side is more problematic for me, honestly. The restlessness is uncomfortable and it can be annoying, but the thing that actually pushed me to make this appointment was realizing how often I’m starting work-related follow-up tasks and then somehow not actually completing them.\n\nAfter audits or mentorship events, I’ll begin the follow-up with every intention of finishing it. I’ll open the email, start the review note, pull up the form, whatever it is, and in my mind I’m handling it. But then days later I’ll discover the final email never got sent, or the review note is still sitting there unfinished, or the form was never submitted. And that’s the part that worries me, because it looks like I didn’t care or didn’t follow through, when really I thought I was taking care of it.\n\nSo yes, if I had to choose, the incomplete tasks and losing track of the final step are more disruptive for me than the physical restlessness.\n\nDoctor: Do you often engage in impulsive behaviors outside of work or speaking, such as sudden, unplanned purchases or reckless driving?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so — at least, not in a way that really stands out to me.\n\nWith purchases, I mean, I can buy something unplanned like anyone does, especially if I’m already online or in a store, but I wouldn’t say I’m making big sudden purchases that get me into trouble or anything like that. It’s not like I’m buying cars or expensive things on a whim. I might get some little thing and later think, “Did I really need that?” but it doesn’t feel like a major pattern.\n\nAnd reckless driving, no, I don’t really think of myself that way. I’m pretty cautious, honestly. I don’t have this sense that I’m taking big risks for the thrill of it.\n\nSo outside of work or conversation-type situations, I’m not sure I’d describe myself as especially impulsive. The issues I notice more are in the things I’ve already mentioned, where I move away from something or act before I’ve really thought it through in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity are interfering with your daily life and relationships to a significant degree?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, unfortunately. I don’t really like saying that, because for a long time I sort of told myself it was just stress or being busy, or that everyone drops things sometimes. But it’s gotten to the point where it’s not just a private annoyance anymore.\n\nAt work, the unfinished follow-up and little mistakes have started to have real consequences. There was a client review that was affected because I didn’t complete something the way I thought I had, or I left a final step hanging, and that was really upsetting. I keep worrying that people are looking at me differently now, like they’re wondering whether I can actually handle the promotion or the level of responsibility I’m supposed to be taking on. And I hate that, because I do care, and I’m not careless in the sense of not trying. But from the outside, I know it can look like I’m unreliable.\n\nAnd at home it’s affecting things too. My husband has had to take over bills and commitments that I agreed to handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. I’ll say, “Yes, I’ll take care of that,” and I mean it when I say it, but then it doesn’t always get done, or it gets done late, or I need reminders. That creates tension, because I think he feels like he has to manage me, and I feel embarrassed and defensive because I don’t want to be that person.\n\nSo yes, I would say it’s significant. It’s affecting how people rely on me, both at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever led to the end of a significant relationship?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean… not the end of a significant relationship, no.\n\nThere have definitely been strains, especially with my husband, where he gets frustrated or feels like he can’t rely on me for certain things, and I can see how that wears on him. But it hasn’t led to a breakup or divorce or anything like that. And looking back on past relationships, I can’t point to one and say, “That ended because of these issues.” I’m sure some of my habits probably annoyed people or caused friction, but I wouldn’t say they were the main reason a significant relationship ended.\n\nSo, no — relationship tension, yes, but not an actual relationship ending because of it, at least not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Did you often have trouble paying attention or following instructions when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, when I look back, it was there pretty early, even in elementary school.\n\nMy report cards would say things like I was bright, or that I understood the material, but then there would always be these comments about needing reminders, or not finishing work, or losing assignments. It wasn’t that I couldn’t do the work — that’s what was confusing, I think, for my teachers and my parents. I could do well on a test if I was sitting there in front of it, but then I’d forget to turn in a project that was literally in my backpack, or I’d rush through something and miss small directions.\n\nMy mother still jokes about that, actually — that I could ace a test but forget the assignment sitting right there with me. At the time it probably sounded funny, but it was also frustrating because I’d get in trouble for things that seemed careless, and I didn’t really know how to explain that I hadn’t meant to ignore the instructions or lose the paper.\n\nAnd I also remember being told I talked out of turn, or needed to be reminded to finish what I was working on. So yes, paying attention to the whole process — not just understanding the lesson, but following all the steps and details — was definitely an issue when I was a child.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’ll say, “Yes, I’ll take care of that,” and I mean it when I say it, but then it doesn’t always get done, or it gets done late, or I need reminders. That creates tension, because I think he feels like he has to manage me, and I feel embarrassed and defensive because I don’t want to be that person.\n\nSo yes, I would say it’s significant. It’s affecting how people rely on me, both at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever led to the end of a significant relationship?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean… not the end of a significant relationship, no.\n\nThere have definitely been strains, especially with my husband, where he gets frustrated or feels like he can’t rely on me for certain things, and I can see how that wears on him. But it hasn’t led to a breakup or divorce or anything like that. And looking back on past relationships, I can’t point to one and say, “That ended because of these issues.” I’m sure some of my habits probably annoyed people or caused friction, but I wouldn’t say they were the main reason a significant relationship ended.\n\nSo, no — relationship tension, yes, but not an actual relationship ending because of it, at least not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Did you often have trouble paying attention or following instructions when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, when I look back, it was there pretty early, even in elementary school.\n\nMy report cards would say things like I was bright, or that I understood the material, but then there would always be these comments about needing reminders, or not finishing work, or losing assignments. It wasn’t that I couldn’t do the work — that’s what was confusing, I think, for my teachers and my parents. I could do well on a test if I was sitting there in front of it, but then I’d forget to turn in a project that was literally in my backpack, or I’d rush through something and miss small directions.\n\nMy mother still jokes about that, actually — that I could ace a test but forget the assignment sitting right there with me. At the time it probably sounded funny, but it was also frustrating because I’d get in trouble for things that seemed careless, and I didn’t really know how to explain that I hadn’t meant to ignore the instructions or lose the paper.\n\nAnd I also remember being told I talked out of turn, or needed to be reminded to finish what I was working on. So yes, paying attention to the whole process — not just understanding the lesson, but following all the steps and details — was definitely an issue when I was a child.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever lost a friend or had difficulty maintaining friendships because of your tendency to forget things, lose focus, or act impulsively?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / accountant_or_auditor / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost a friend because of it. Not in the sense of someone saying, “I can’t be friends with you anymore because you forgot things,” or anything that direct.

I have had friendships where I worried I wasn’t doing my part very well — like forgetting to respond to messages, or meaning to follow up and then realizing way too much time had passed. And then I feel awkward, so sometimes I delay even more because I don’t know how to explain why I disappeared. But whether that actually caused the friendship to fade, I don’t know. It’s hard to separate that from normal adult life, people getting busy, kids, work, moving away, all of that.

I don’t think I’ve had a dramatic friendship ending because of it. More like… I’m aware that I may not be the easiest person to keep up with sometimes, and I’ve felt guilty about that. But I can’t point to a specific friend I lost for that reason.
